The four verses are taken from the Fifth Biblical Ode, the Canticle of the Prophet Isaiah (Isaiah 26:9–15 LXX): (1) **"From the night my spirit waketh early unto Thee, O God, for Thy commandments are a light upon the earth"** (v. 9); (2) **"Learn righteousness, ye that dwell upon the earth"** (v. 9); (3) **"Jealousy shall seize upon an untaught people, and now fire shall devour the adversaries"** (v. 11); and (4) **"Add evils unto them, O Lord, add evils unto them, unto the glorious of the earth"** (v. 15). This ancient penitential substitution emphasizes vigilance, judgment, and compunction, replacing the festive joy of the dawn with ascetic longing for the light of Christ.
[^81]: The Memorial Alleluia of Saturday and the Ancestral Soul Saturdays (*Σάββατα τῶν Ψυχῶν*) (Violakis, pp. 64–65, §19). On ordinary Saturdays throughout the year that lack the commemoration of an illustrious Saint, the ancestral Sabbath character of resting in Christ is manifested by replacing **"God is the Lord"** with the Memorial **"Alleluia"** in Tone 2, accompanied by the appointed verses for the departed: **"Blessed are they whom Thou hast chosen and taken to Thyself, O Lord"** (Psalm 64:5 LXX) and **"And their memorial is from generation to generation"** (Psalm 101:13 LXX). These verses are followed by the general troparia for martyrs and the departed: **"Apostles, Martyrs, and Prophets"** and **"Remember, O Lord, as Thou art good, Thy servants."** On the two universal Saturdays of Souls (*Σάββατα τῶν Ψυχῶν*)—Meatfare Saturday and the Saturday before Pentecost—the services are dedicated entirely to the memory of all departed Orthodox Christians from the ages, featuring the classic funeral Apolytikion in Tone 8: **"O Thou Who with profound wisdom and love for mankind orderest all things..."** (*Ὁ βάθει σοφίας*).
[^82]: The Liturgical Stichologia of the Psalter and the Polyeleos vs. Amomos Distinction (Violakis, pp. 66–67, §20). In §20, Violakis details the ancient monastic and cathedral discipline regarding the Psalter at Matins. Traditionally, the appointed Kathismata of the Psalter are chanted in verses (*stichologountai*) prior to the Sessional Hymns (Kathismata) of each of the two (or three) stichologiae. By pastoral economy (*oikonomia chronou*), contemporary parish practice often transfers the reading of the prescribed Kathismata of the Psalter, the Polyeleos, or the Amomos (Psalm 118 LXX) before the beginning of Matins. Violakis delineates the scriptural structure of the Polyeleos (*Πολυέλεος*): for Feasts of the Lord, Psalms 134 and 135 LXX (**"Praise the Name of the Lord"** and **"Give thanks unto the Lord, for He is good"**); for Feasts of the Mother of God, Psalm 44 LXX (**"My heart hath poured forth a good word"**); and for celebrated Saints, proper festal selections from the *Eklogarion* (or Psalms 134–135 LXX as default). On the Pre-Lenten Meatfare and Cheesefare Sundays, Psalm 136 LXX (**"By the rivers of Babylon"** [*Ἐπὶ τῶν ποταμῶν Βαβυλῶνος*]) is added to evoke the sorrow of exile from Paradise. The annual distribution mandates two Kathismata daily and three during Great Lent, culminating in completing the Psalter twice during the First Week of Lent, and once between Palm Sunday Vespers and Holy Wednesday. In accordance with universal Byzantine typika, psalmody ceases entirely from Holy Thursday until Saturday of Bright Week.
[^83]: The Architecture of Sessional Hymns (Kathismata) at Matins (Violakis, pp. 66–69, §21). Paragraph 21 establishes the rules governing the hymnographic Kathismata (*Καθίσματα*, Sessional Hymns) that follow the Psalter stichologiae at Matins. On Sundays coinciding with Marian Feasts, the first two stichologiae combine two Resurrection Kathismata and one Festal Kathisma, displacing the ordinary Octoechos Theotokia; the third stichologia is reserved exclusively for the Festal Kathisma following the Polyeleos. On Afterfeast Sundays and Apodoses, the third stichologia Kathisma is suppressed, and festal troparia replace the Octoechos Theotokia at the first two stichologiae. On commemorations of celebrated Saints with Polyeleos (the Holy Apostles, the Forerunner, the Three Hierarchs, Great Martyrs), the Saint's Kathisma is intercalated into each of the first two stichologiae after the Resurrection Kathisma and before the Theotokion, and the full third stichologia is chanted following the Polyeleos.
[^84]: The Sunday Resurrectional Complex: Evlogitaria, Hypakoe, Anavathmoi, and Matins Gospel (Violakis, pp. 68–69, §22). In §22, Violakis codifies the core resurrectional sequence of Sunday Matins: following the Kathismata, the Resurrectional Evlogitaria (**"Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes"** [*Εὐλογητὸς εἶ, Κύριε*], Psalm 118:12 LXX) are chanted, followed by the Little Litany, the sacerdotal exclamation **"For blessed is Thy Name..."**, the Hypakoe (*Ὑπακοή*) of the Tone, the Gradual Antiphons (*Ἀναβαθμοί*), the Sunday Prokimenon, and the Canons. If a Feast of the Mother of God coincides with Sunday, the Evlogitaria are omitted, and the order transitions directly to the Little Litany, Hypakoe, Gradual Antiphons, and the Festal Prokimenon. In this festal order, the Matins Gospel of the Feast is proclaimed, followed by **"Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ"** (*Ἀνάστασιν Χριστοῦ θεασάμενοι*), Psalm 50 LXX read *chuma* (smoothly/non-melodically), **"Through the intercessions of the Theotokos"**, and the festal Idiomelon. On Afterfeasts and Apodoses of Dominical or Marian Feasts falling on Sunday, however, the resurrectional Eothinon (Morning) Gospel of the weekly cycle takes precedence and is strictly preserved.
[^85]: Resurrectional Primacy of the Octoechos Canons and Seasonal Exceptions (Violakis, pp. 68–71, §23). Paragraph 23 sets forth the inviolable canonical principle that the Sunday Resurrection Canons of the Octoechos are never wholly omitted, even when coinciding with Marian Feasts or Apodoses. Violakis catalogs the specific historical exceptions: the Third Sunday of Lent (Veneration of the Holy Cross) and the Sunday before Christmas (Holy Fathers/Genealogy), where the Canons of the day replace the Octoechos Canons, but their initial troparia following the Heirmos are resurrectional and intoned with the verse **"Glory to Thy holy Resurrection, O Lord."** Similarly, on the four Sundays following Thomas Sunday (Myrrhbearers, Paralytic, Samaritan Woman, Blind Man), the Octoechos Canons are superseded by the Paschal Canon of St. John of Damascus with its two Theotokia. When a Marian Feast or Apodosis falls on Sunday, the Octoechos Canon is chanted first to four verses, followed by the festal Canon (or Canons to twelve verses). For weekday commemorations of Saints, the Small Paraklesis Canon of the Theotokos is chanted alongside the Saint's Canon, except for twelve major feast days of Apostles, Evangelists, and Hierarchs which possess proper Marian Canons or Pentecostarion/festal antecedents.
[^86]: From Biblical Canticles to Troparion Refrains: The Evolution of Canon Stichologia (Violakis, pp. 70–73, §24). Violakis documents the liturgical transition from the ancient practice of chanting the Nine Biblical Odes (*αἱ ἐννέα Ὠδαί*)—the canticles of Moses, Hannah, Habakkuk, Isaiah, Jonah, the Three Youths, the Magnificat, and the Benedictus—to the abbreviated modern usage where the biblical verses are replaced by repetitive stichic refrains prefixed to each troparion. The canonical refrains are: **"Glory to Thy holy Resurrection, O Lord"** for Resurrection Canons; **"Most Holy Theotokos, save us"** for Marian Canons; **"Saints of God, intercede for us"** for Saints' Canons; **"Have mercy on me, O God, have mercy on me"** for the Great Penitential Canon of St. Andrew of Crete; and selected verses from Psalms 64, 24, and 101 LXX for funeral troparia. On Great Feasts of the Lord, because the biblical canticles are omitted entirely, no verses are sung before the troparia, save for **"Glory..."** and **"Now and forever..."** before the final two troparia. Throughout Afterfeasts of Dominical Feasts, the refrain **"Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee"** is prefixed to the festal troparia.
[^87]: Liturgical Articulation at the Third Ode: Kathisma and Kontakion Precedence (Violakis, pp. 72–73, §25). The conclusion of the Third Ode of the Canon marks an ancient liturgical junction at Matins. Following the Little Litany and the priestly exclamation, the Kathisma (Sessional Hymn) and Theotokion of the Saint are chanted. If a Feast of the Mother of God falls on Sunday, the Kontakion and Ikos of the Octoechos are transferred to the Third Ode and read prior to the Festal Kathisma, ensuring that the resurrectional commemoration is preserved while granting the post-ode sessional hymnody to the Feast. The same transfer rule governs all Sundays of the Triodion and commemorations of major celebrated Saints. On Forefeast and Afterfeast Sundays, the Kathisma of the Saint is chanted first, followed by that of the Forefeast or Afterfeast.
[^88]: The Climax of the Canon: The Sixth Ode Kontakion and Ikos Transfer Rules (Violakis, pp. 72–75, §26). Paragraph 26 defines the placement of the Kontakion and Ikos at the conclusion of the Sixth Ode of Matins, preceded by the Little Litany and the exclamation **"For Thou art the King of peace..."** (*Ὅτι Σὺ εἶ ὁ Βασιλεὺς τῆς εἰρήνης*). On ordinary Sundays, the Kontakion and Ikos of the Octoechos are read. Violakis catalogs the extensive system of festal exceptions where the Octoechos Kontakion is displaced by that of the feast or seasonal commemoration: Marian Feasts, Triodion Sundays (except the 2nd, 4th, and 5th Sundays of Lent, which retain the Octoechos), the Pentecostarion Sundays, the Ancestral and Pre-Nativity Sundays (Forefathers, Sunday before/after Nativity), the Synods of the Holy Fathers (Nicaea I, Chalcedon, Nicaea II), and major saints whose feasts transfer the Resurrection Kontakion to the Third Ode. Furthermore, during the Pentecostarion, the Kontakia of Saints George, John the Theologian, and Constantine and Helen replace the Resurrection Kontakion at the Sixth Ode whenever their feasts coincide with a Sunday (excluding Thomas Sunday, Pentecost, and All Saints).
[^89]: The Katavasia Cycle: "I Shall Open My Mouth" and the Seasonal Typikon Stations (Violakis, pp. 74–75, §27). In §27, Violakis outlines the annual distribution of the Katavasiae (*Καταβασίαι*)—the concluding heirmoi sung by both choirs descending (*katabainein*) to the center of the nave. The normative Marian Katavasiae in Tone 4, **"I shall open my mouth, and it shall be filled with the Spirit"** (*Ἀνοίξω τὸ στόμα μου*), serve as the foundational default for all non-festal intervals of the liturgical year: (1) from the Apodosis of the Universal Exaltation of the Holy Cross (September 21) until the Forefeast of the Entry of the Theotokos (November 21); (2) from the Apodosis of the Meeting of the Lord (February 9) until the beginning of the Triodion, as well as on the 2nd, 4th, and 5th Sundays of Lent; and (3) from the Sunday of All Saints until July 27. On Sundays within Forefeasts and Afterfeasts, proper festal Katavasiae supersede the general cycle. On the first four Sundays of the Triodion and the First Sunday of Lent, the Triodion Katavasiae are chanted. On weekdays of the Triodion when a Saint occurs, the Katavasiae **"I shall open my mouth"** are chanted, and the Marian Kontakion **"O Protection of Christians unashamed"** (*Προστασία τῶν Χριστιανῶν*) is appointed for the Divine Liturgy.
[^90]: The Proclamation of the Resurrectional Morning Gospel at Sunday Matins and the Sacerdotal Intercession (*Σῶσον ὁ Θεός*) (Violakis, pp. 76–77, §28, Note 10). In §28, Violakis details the solemn liturgical complex following the Katavasia of the Eighth Ode on ordinary Sundays and Sundays of the Triodion. The Deacon (or Priest) opens the rite with the invitation **"Let us pray to the Lord"**, followed by the sacerdotal exclamation **"For Thou art holy, our God, and Thou dwellest among the saints..."** (*Ὅτι ἅγιος εἶ, ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν*), and the antiphonal proclamation of the Morning Prokimenon and **"Let every breath praise the Lord"** (*Πᾶσα πνοὴ αἰνεσάτω τὸν Κύριον*). The Priest proclaims the appointed Resurrectional Eothinon Gospel (from the fixed eleven-pericope cycle of the Resurrection apparitions), followed immediately by the ancient baptismal hymn **"Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ"** (*Ἀνάστασιν Χριστοῦ θεασάμενοι*), Psalm 50 LXX (**"Have mercy on me, O God"**), and the Pentecostal-resurrectional troparia (**"Through the intercessions of the Apostles... Through the intercessions of the Theotokos... Jesus, having risen from the grave..."**). On Sundays of the Triodion, these troparia are replaced by the compunctious penitential hymns of St. Andrew of Crete and St. Theodore the Studite: **"The doors of repentance do Thou open unto me, O Giver of Life"** (*Τῆς μετανοίας ἄνοιξόν μοι πύλας, Ζωοδότα*), **"Guide me in the paths of salvation, O Theotokos"**, and **"When I ponder the multitude of my evil deeds..."**. In Note 10, Violakis addresses a widespread liturgical abuse and pastoral anomaly: the sacerdotal intercession **"O God, save Thy people, and bless Thine inheritance..."** (*Σῶσον, ὁ Θεός, τὸν λαόν σου*) and its subsequent exclamation **"Through the mercies and compassions..."** belong strictly and canonically to the presiding Priest according to ancient Byzantine rubrics. However, because the Priest is frequently occupied in the Prothesis chapel performing the rite of Preparation (*Proskomide* / *Προσκομιδή*), this prayer was often delegated by concession to the Deacon. Violakis sternly reasserts the canonical principle: the rubrical order must be preserved (*ἡ τάξις ὅμως τηρητέα*), and the Priest is obliged to interrupt his preparation of the Holy Gifts at that precise liturgical junction, exit the Sanctuary or stand before the Holy Table, and personally intone the solemn intercession.
[^91]: The Stichologia of the Ninth Ode: The Biblical Canticle of the Theotokos vs. Festal Megalynaria (Violakis, pp. 76–79, §29). Paragraph 29 delineates the structural execution of the Ninth Ode at Matins. On normative days throughout the year—including ordinary Sundays, commemorations of celebrated Saints, Afterfeasts, and weekdays from Thomas Sunday to Pentecost—the Ninth Ode begins with the biblical Canticle of the Mother of God, the Magnificat (Luke 1:46–55), interspersed with the venerable Megalynarion composed by St. Cosmas the Melodist: **"More honorable than the Cherubim, and beyond compare more glorious than the Seraphim..."** (*Τὴν Τιμιωτέραν τῶν Χερουβίμ*), intoned according to the Tone of the day's Katavasiae. Violakis outlines the systematic exceptions where the Magnificat is suppressed in favor of festal Megalynaria (*Μεγαλυνάρια*) intercalated directly between the troparia of the Ninth Ode of the festal Canon: (1) Great Feasts of the Lord and of the Theotokos; (2) Holy Week; and (3) all Sundays from Pascha until Ascension. On the Apodoses (Leave-takings) of Dominical Feasts, the Ninth Ode is sung without **"More honorable"**, except for the Exaltation of the Holy Cross (September 14) and the Transfiguration (August 6) according to the Typikon of Constantine, which lack proper festal Megalynaria. On Marian Apodoses, if coinciding with Sunday, the Magnificat precedes the festal Ninth Ode and its Megalynaria; on weekdays, the festal Ninth Ode stands alone. Crucial idiosyncratic distinctions govern two major feasts: on the Apodosis of the Meeting of the Lord (February 9), because all fourteen festal Megalynaria are chanted, the Magnificat is completely displaced; on the Apodosis of the Annunciation (March 26), only the Magnificat is sung with the Heirmos and troparia, without festal Megalynaria. Finally, on the Feast of the Three Hierarchs (January 30), all three Ninth Odes are chanted in their entirety without the Magnificat, conforming to the Studite poly-heortological cathedral tradition (clarified in Violakis p. 167, §4, Note 4).
[^92]: The Sunday Trinitarian Doxology (*Ἅγιος Κύριος ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν*) and the Hierarchy of Exapostilaria (Violakis, pp. 78–81, §30, Notes * and 11). Paragraph 30 codifies the rubrics governing the Exapostilaria (*Ἐξαποστειλάρια*, hymns of "sending forth" at the dawn) and their introductory Trinitarian acclamation at Matins. Violakis establishes that the solemn singing of **"Holy is the Lord our God"** (*Ἅγιος Κύριος ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν*, Psalm 98:5, 9 LXX) is a unique, inviolable hallmark of Sunday Resurrectional Matins, never sung on weekdays even when commemorating celebrated Saints. On Sundays coinciding with Marian Feasts or Apodoses, the Sunday Resurrection Exapostilarion is chanted first, followed by the festal Exapostilarion twice. On Afterfeasts with celebrated Saints, the threefold progression is observed: (1) Resurrection, (2) Saint, and (3) Afterfeast. In Note 11, Violakis explains the performance practice of **"Holy is the Lord our God"**: it is chanted twice in the Tone of the Sunday Resurrection Exapostilarion, followed by the verse **"Exalt the Lord our God, and worship at His footstool, for He is holy"** (Psalm 98:5 LXX), whereafter the choir intones the Exapostilarion itself. On Palm Sunday and Holy Saturday, however, because neither service possesses a proper hymnographic Exapostilarion, **"Holy is the Lord our God"** is chanted three times without the psalm verse, functioning as the Exapostilarion itself. On Pascha and throughout Bright Week, the Paschal Exapostilarion **"Having fallen asleep in the flesh as a mortal..."** (*Σαρκὶ ὑπνώσας*) is chanted immediately without **"Holy is the Lord"**. During the Paschal season, Thomas Sunday features proper Exapostilaria: **"With thy hand, Thomas, probe My wounds..."** (*Ἐμῶν μελῶν χειρί σου*) and **"Today spring is fragrant..."** (*Σήμερον ἔαρ μυρίζει*), intercalating the Saint's Exapostilarion between them when one occurs.
[^93]: The Stichologia of the Praises (*Αἶνοι*): Combining Resurrectional, Festal, and Paschal Hymnody (Violakis, pp. 80–83, §31). In §31, Violakis details the numerical distribution of the Stichira at the Praises (*Ainoi*, Psalms 148, 149, 150 LXX) on Sundays coinciding with diverse liturgical cycles. The standard total allotment is eight stichira (intoned with the final four verses of Psalm 148/149 and four verses of Psalm 150 LXX). On Sundays of Marian Feasts or Apodoses, the choir divides the stichira equally: four Resurrectional Stichira of the Octoechos and four festal Stichira. When a celebrated Saint coincides with an Afterfeast or Apodosis Sunday, the tripartite distribution applies: three Resurrectional, two Afterfeast, and three of the Saint. If the Meeting of the Lord falls on one of the first four Sundays of the Triodion, Violakis distinguishes between the preparatory Sundays (Publican & Pharisee, Prodigal Son: 4 Resurrection + 4 Feast) and the strict pre-lenten Sundays (Meatfare, Cheesefare: 3 Resurrection + 2 Triodion + 3 Feast), preserving the thematic transition to Great Lent. For the Feast of the Annunciation falling on the Third Sunday of Lent (Veneration of the Cross), the Cross takes precedence: 2 Resurrection + 3 Cross + 3 Annunciation; if falling on the Fourth or Fifth Sunday of Lent, 4 Resurrection + 4 Annunciation. On the four Sundays following Thomas Sunday (Myrrhbearers, Paralytic, Samaritan Woman, Blind Man), commemorations of major saints (St. George, St. John the Theologian, Saints Constantine and Helen) introduce the Paschal Stichira: 3 Resurrection + 3 Saint, followed by the joyous Paschal Stichira **"A sacred Pascha hath been revealed to us today"** (*Πάσχα ἱερὸν ἡμῖν σήμερον ἀναδέδεικται*) with their proper Paschal verses (**"Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered..."**).
[^94]: The Eothinon Doxastikon of the Praises and Festal Displacements in Byzantine Cathedral Usage (Violakis, pp. 82–85, §32). Paragraph 32 codifies the theological and hymnographic role of the Eothinon Doxastikon (*Ἑωθινὸν Δοξαστικόν*)—the solemn hymn chanted at **"Glory to the Father..."** before the Great Doxology at Sunday Matins. Composed by the Byzantine Emperor Leo VI the Wise (886–912), the eleven Eothina Doxastika provide a profound liturgical commentary and theological synopsis of the eleven Sunday Morning Gospels. Under normative conditions, the Eothinon Doxastikon is chanted whenever the corresponding Morning Gospel has been proclaimed. Violakis catalogs the systematic exceptions where the Eothinon is displaced by a festal or hagiographical Doxastikon: (1) Feasts and Apodoses of the Theotokos and of the Lord; (2) all Sundays of the Triodion and Pentecostarion; (3) Pre-Nativity and Post-Nativity Sundays; (4) the Sundays of the Holy Fathers of the Ecumenical Councils (Nicaea I, Chalcedon, Nicaea II); (5) major feast days of the Forerunner, Apostles, Evangelists, and Hierarchs; and (6) patronal and titular feasts of the local temple. Significantly, Violakis records the authoritative ruling of Patriarch Constantius I of Constantinople (1830–1834): the Doxastika of St. Gregory the Theologian (January 25), St. John Chrysostom (November 13 / January 27), St. Andrew the First-Called (November 30), and the Great Martyr Euphemia (September 16 / July 11) must also supersede the Sunday Eothinon when falling on a Sunday, specifically honoring the Apostolic founder, the great Patriarchal Doctor-Fathers, and the Patroness and miracle-worker of the Ecumenical Patriarchate in Constantinople whose relics reside in the Patriarchal Church of St. George.
[^95]: The Marian Doxasticon Theotokion: "Most Blessed Art Thou" vs. the Paschal "Day of Resurrection" (Violakis, pp. 84–85, §33). Paragraph 33 governs the concluding Theotokion chanted at **"Now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** following the Doxastikon of the Praises and immediately preceding the Great Doxology. The universal Byzantine norm for Sunday Matins is the venerable Dogmatic Theotokion in Tone 2: **"Most blessed art thou, O Virgin Theotokos; for through Him Who was incarnate of thee, Hades is taken captive..."** (*Ὑπερευλογημένη ὑπάρχεις, Θεοτόκε Παρθένε*). Violakis notes that this hymn is so foundational to the Sunday resurrectional mystery that it is preserved even when a Feast of the Mother of God, or its Apodosis, or the Apodosis of a Feast of the Lord occurs on Sunday. The sole universal exception occurs during the post-Paschal season: on the four Sundays following Thomas Sunday (Myrrhbearers, Paralytic, Samaritan Woman, and Blind Man), **"Most blessed art thou"** is replaced by the climactic Paschal doxasticon composed by St. John of Damascus: **"The Day of Resurrection! Let us beam with festive joy, and let us embrace one another..."** (*Ἀναστάσεως ἡμέρα, καὶ λαμπρυνθῶμεν τῇ πανηγύρει*). When major Saints occur on these post-Paschal Sundays, the Sunday Theotokion is sung at **"Now and forever..."**, except on Myrrhbearers Sunday, where **"The Day of Resurrection"** is retained because the day's Doxastikon is itself an Eothinon hymn. On weekdays from Thomas Sunday until the Apodosis of Pascha, commemorations of these Saints appoint the Doxastikon of the preceding Sunday at **"Now and forever..."** rather than the Paschal hymn, preserving the strict hierarchical distinction between Lord's Days and ordinary ferias.
[^96]: The Marian Theotokia of the Praises across the Triodion, Holy Week, and Paschalion (Violakis, pp. 86–87, §33 cont.). Continuing the rubrics of §33, Violakis addresses complex intersections of the movable and fixed cycles at the conclusion of Sunday Matins Praises (*Ainoi*). When the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (February 2) falls on one of the first four Sundays of the Triodion (Publican and Pharisee, Prodigal Son, Meatfare, or Cheesefare), at **"Glory..."** the appointed Doxastikon of the Triodion is sung, followed at **"Now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** by the festal Doxastikon of the Meeting. When the Feast of the Annunciation (March 25) coincides with the Saturday of Lazarus, Palm Sunday, or Holy Thursday, the Triodion Doxastikon is chanted at **"Glory..."**, and the festal Doxastikon of the Annunciation is intoned at **"Now and forever..."**; however, if the Annunciation falls on Pascha itself or any day of Bright Week (Kyriopascha), the Annunciation Doxastikon is intoned at **"Glory..."**, followed at **"Now and forever..."** by the Paschal Doxastikon: **"The Day of Resurrection! Let us beam with festive joy..."** (*Ἀναστάσεως ἡμέρα*). On ferias commemorating celebrated Saints outside the Pentecostarion period, when no Forefeast or Afterfeast coincides, after the Saint's Doxastikon at **"Glory..."**, the choir chants at **"Now and forever..."** a weekday Theotokion in the same Tone, drawn from the Horologion cycle of weekday Marian hymns rather than Sunday Resurrectional Theotokia.
[^97]: The Dichotomy of the Great and Small Doxology and the Paschal Substitution (Violakis, pp. 86–87, §34). Paragraph 34 establishes the structural typology of the Doxology at Matins. On all Sundays, Dominical Feasts, Marian Feasts, and commemorations of celebrated Saints with Polyeleos or Doxology rank, the Great Doxology (*Μεγάλη Δοξολογία*) is chanted solemnly by the choirs in the Tone of the preceding Doxastikon of the Praises, terminating with the Trisagion sung with prolonged, melismatic melody. Conversely, on ordinary ferias, fasting days of Great Lent, and the first five days of Holy Week (from Matins of Holy Monday through Matins of Holy Friday), the Small Doxology **"To Thee belongeth glory, O Lord our God, and unto Thee do we ascribe glory: to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit..."** (*Σοὶ δόξα πρέπει*) is read simply in a speaking voice, immediately followed by the Litany of Fervent Supplication (*Ektenia*) and the Aposticha. During the entire renewal of Bright Week and on the Apodosis of Pascha, both the Great and Small Doxologies are completely suppressed; in their stead, immediately following **"The Day of Resurrection"**, the choirs chant the Paschal troparion **"Christ is risen from the dead, trampling down death by death, and upon those in the tombs bestowing life"** (*Χριστὸς ἀνέστη*) thrice with its festive melody, which fulfills the liturgical role of both the Doxology and the post-Doxological dismissal troparion.
[^98]: The Sunday Matins Troparion *“Today Salvation”* and Post-Doxological Dismissal Hymnody (Violakis, pp. 86–89, §35). Paragraph 35 codifies the variable troparia chanted immediately following the Great Doxology at Sunday Matins. In Byzantine and Constantinopolitan usage codified by Violakis, on all Sundays throughout the year—including Sundays coinciding with Marian Feasts, Apodoses of Dominical or Marian Feasts, Afterfeasts, Sundays of the Triodion (excluding Palm Sunday), and the four post-Paschal Sundays of the Pentecostarion (Myrrhbearers, Paralytic, Samaritan Woman, Blind Man), as well as on Lazarus Saturday—the choir chants the Resurrectional dismissal troparion **"Today salvation has come to the world; let us sing unto Him Who is risen from the grave, the Author of our life; for having destroyed death by death, He hath given us the victory and great mercy"** (*Σήμερον σωτηρία τῷ κόσμῳ γέγονεν*) in the Tone of the day. In broader Studite and Ruthenian practice, this hymn is alternated with the troparion **"Having risen from the tomb and burst the bonds of Hades..."** (*Ἀναστὰς ἐκ τοῦ μνήματος*), where *“Today Salvation”* is sung on even Tones (Tones 2, 4, 6, 8) and *“Having risen”* on odd Tones (Tones 1, 3, 5, 7); Violakis here presents the strict cathedral use wherein *“Today Salvation”* serves as the standard post-Doxological hymn for the Sunday resurrectional office. On ferias coinciding with Dominical, Marian, or celebrated Saints' feasts, the proper Dismissal Troparion of the Feast or Saint is sung at the conclusion of Matins. During Bright Week and on the Apodosis of Pascha, **"Christ is risen"** thrice displaces this troparion entirely. From Thomas Sunday until the Apodosis of Pascha (excepting the Afterfeast of Mid-Pentecost), weekday saint commemorations take the Dismissal Troparion of the preceding Sunday at the dismissal of Matins. Finally, on ordinary ferias lacking a feast, the daily dismissal Theotokion from the Horologion matching the Tone of the daily Dismissal Troparion is said.
[^99]: The Typika and Beatitudes in the Divine Liturgy (Violakis, pp. 90–91, §36). Paragraph 36 regulates the introductory Psalmody and Makarismoi (*Μακαρισμοί*) of the Divine Liturgy. On every Sunday, the standard Eucharistic entrance psalmody consists of the Typika: Psalm 102 LXX (**"Bless the Lord, O my soul"**), Psalm 145 LXX (**"Praise the Lord, O my soul"**), the hymn **"Only-Begotten Son and Immortal Word of God"** (*Ὁ μονογενὴς Υἱὸς*), and the Beatitudes (Matthew 5:3–12 LXX) intoned with eight Resurrection Troparia from the Octoechos (four from the Resurrection Canon and four from the Cross and Resurrection Canon, or selected according to the Tone). When a Forefeast, Afterfeast, or commemoration of a celebrated Saint falls on a Sunday, the eightfold allotment of the Beatitudes is apportioned: four Resurrection Troparia of the Beatitudes are retained, and four troparia are intercalated from the Sixth Ode of the festal Canon (or Canon of the Saint), ensuring that the hymnographic memory of the feast enters into the solemn Eucharistic proclamation of the Gospel Beatitudes.
[^100]: The Hierarchy of Festal and Weekday Antiphons in the Divine Liturgy (Violakis, pp. 90–93, §37). Paragraph 37 defines the application of Antiphons (*Ἀντίφωνα*) at the Divine Liturgy across the liturgical year. On Great Feasts of the Lord, proper festal Antiphons derived from thematic Psalms are chanted with specific refrains on whatever day the feast falls; when a Dominical Apodosis falls on Sunday, the Second Antiphon preserves the proper festal refrain rather than the Sunday resurrectional refrain. On Marian Feasts or their Apodoses coinciding with Sunday, the Second Antiphon adopts the resurrectional refrain: **"Save us, O Son of God, Who art risen from the dead, who sing unto Thee: Alleluia"** (*Σῶσον ἡμᾶς Υἱὲ Θεοῦ ὁ ἀναστὰς ἐκ νεκρῶν*); on weekdays, however, it takes the hagiographical refrain: **"Save us, O Son of God, Who art wonderful in the saints..."** (*ὁ ἐν Ἁγίοις θαυμαστός*), with proper seasonal variations for the Meeting of the Lord (**"...Who wast borne in the arms of the righteous Simeon"**) and the Annunciation (**"...Who wast incarnate for our sake"**). Violakis observes that while contemporary usage sings the Paschal Antiphons daily until Ascension, ancient Typika restored the Typika and Beatitudes beginning immediately on Thomas Sunday. For celebrated Saints on weekdays, the Typika are obligatory because saints possess no proper Antiphons. On simple weekdays, the three daily Antiphons are drawn from Psalms 91, 92, and 94 LXX with their respective refrains: **"Through the intercessions of the Theotokos..."**, **"Through the intercessions of Thy Saints..."**, and **"Save us, O Son of God, Who art wonderful in the saints..."**
[^101]: The Eisodika (Entrance Hymns) of the Little Entrance and the Annunciation Apodosis (Violakis, pp. 92–95, §38, Note 12). Paragraph 38 details the chants of the Little Entrance (*Μικρὰ Εἴσοδος*) with the Holy Gospel at the Divine Liturgy. The universal Sunday and Marian norm is the ancient entrance chant based on Psalm 94:6 LXX: **"O come, let us worship and bow down before Christ. Save us, O Son of God, Who art risen from the dead, who sing unto Thee: Alleluia"** (*Δεῦτε προσκυνήσωμεν καὶ προσπέσωμεν Χριστῷ*). When the Annunciation or Meeting of the Lord falls on a Sunday, their proper festal Eisodikon verses are proclaimed, but the concluding Christological clause must be the resurrectional refrain **"Who art risen from the dead"**; this same rule governs the Apodoses of Dominical Feasts falling on Sunday. On simple weekdays, the refrain is **"Who art wonderful in the saints"**. In Note 12, Violakis explains the canonical status of the Apodosis of the Annunciation (March 26): strictly speaking, the Annunciation does not have a formal multi-day Afterfeast period like other Dominical and Marian feasts; however, when the feast falls on a Friday or Saturday of Great Lent, the liturgical celebration extends into an Apodosis on the following day (Saturday or Sunday) due to the strict fasting and festal rubrics of the Triodion. Finally, Violakis notes the popular post-Byzantine practice of chanting the Paschal Eisodikon **"In the congregations bless ye God, the Lord from the springs of Israel"** (Psalm 67:27 LXX) daily through the Apodosis of Pascha, critiquing it as an inaccurate extension of what was originally restricted to Bright Week.
[^102]: The Hierarchy of Post-Entrance Dismissal Troparia and Sunday Memorial Commemorations (Violakis, pp. 94–95, §39). Paragraph 39 governs the complex order of troparia and kontakia chanted immediately following the Little Entrance at the Divine Liturgy. On Sundays, the Resurrection Troparion of the Octoechos Tone always takes absolute primacy, preceding even the Dismissal Troparia of coinciding Marian Feasts or Dominical Apodoses. On Afterfeast Sundays with a celebrated Saint, the fourfold succession is strictly observed: (1) Resurrection Troparion, (2) Festal Dismissal Troparion, (3) Saint's Dismissal Troparion, and (4) Dismissal Troparion of the Patron Saint of the local temple. On weekdays during Afterfeasts, the order is: (1) Festal Dismissal Troparion, (2) Saint's Dismissal Troparion, and (3) Patron Saint of the Church. From Thomas Sunday until Ascension on weekdays with saints, the Dismissal Troparion of the preceding Sunday leads the sequence. Great Martyrs (such as St. George, St. Demetrius, St. Barbara, St. Catherine) enjoy a unique liturgical privilege: even when occurring on a feria during an Afterfeast without an official vigil or polyeleos commemoration, their Dismissal Troparion may never be omitted. When a Memorial Service (*Panakhida*) for the departed is celebrated in conjunction with Sunday Liturgy, the funeral troparion in Tone 2: **"Remember, O Lord, as Thou art good, Thy servants, and forgive whatsoever sins they have committed in this life; for no one is sinless save Thee Who art able to grant rest unto the departed"** (*Μνήσθητι, Κύριε, ὡς ἀγαθός, τῶν δούλων σου*) is intercalated immediately prior to the Dismissal Troparion of the Patron Saint of the Church.
[^103]: Seasonal Eucharistic Kontakia, Ordinary Ferias, and the Triodion Cycle (Violakis, pp. 96–97, §40 & Note 13). Paragraph 40 establishes the seasonal and weekly hierarchy of the concluding post-Entrance Kontakion (*Κοντάκιον*) at the Divine Liturgy. Outside festal seasons—namely during the general periods of the year (from the Sunday of All Saints until September 1, and from September 22 until November 7)—the universal concluding Kontakion at the Divine Liturgy is the general Marian protectress hymn in Tone 4 (or Tone 2): **"O Protection of Christians unashamed, mediation unto the Creator unshakeable, despise not the prayerful voices of sinners; but in thy goodness hasten to assist us who faithfully cry unto thee: Speed to intercession, and hasten to supplication, O Theotokos, who dost ever protect those who honor thee"** (*Προστασία τῶν Χριστιανῶν ἀκαταίσχυντε*). During Forefeasts, Feasts, and Afterfeasts of Dominical and Marian solemnities, the Kontakion of the feast is intoned at the conclusion of all troparia (e.g., Nativity of the Theotokos, Exaltation of the Precious Cross, Presentation, Nativity of Christ, Theophany, Meeting of the Lord, Annunciation, etc.). In Note 13, Violakis specifies the seasonal distribution of Kontakia during the Triodion: on the preparatory Sundays (Publican and Pharisee, Prodigal Son, Meatfare, Cheesefare) and throughout the Sundays of Great Lent (Sunday of Orthodoxy, St. Gregory Palamas, Veneration of the Precious Cross, St. John Climacus, St. Mary of Egypt), the proper Kontakion of the Triodion (or of the Cross) is chanted as the final Kontakion. On ferias of Great Lent, Saturdays of Great Lent, and Akathist Saturday, the celebrated hymn to the Theotokos **"To Thee, the Champion Leader and Victorious, a hymn of thanksgiving for deliverance from perils do I, Thy city, dedicate unto Thee, O Theotokos..."** (*Τῇ ὑπερμάχῳ στρατηγῷ τὰ νικητήρια*) is appointed. On Lazarus Saturday, Palm Sunday, Holy Thursday, and Holy Saturday, proper Kontakia are appointed. From Bright Week through the Apodosis of Pascha, the Paschal Kontakion **"Though Thou didst descend into the grave, O Immortal One, yet didst Thou destroy the power of Hades..."** (*Εἰ καὶ ἐν τάφῳ κατῆλθες, Ἀθάνατε*) displaces all others. On Ascension, Pentecost, and their Afterfeasts, the respective festal Kontakion is sung.
[^104]: Weekday Daily Kontakia and Annunciation Intersections (Violakis, pp. 100–101, §40 cont.). Paragraph 40 continues with the distribution of daily weekday Kontakia on ferias outside festive seasons. After the Dismissal Troparion of the day and of the commemorated Saint, and that of the Patron Saint of the local temple: on Monday (the Holy Angels), the Kontakion of the Bodiless Powers (**"O Archangels of God, ministers of divine glory..."**); on Tuesday (the Holy Forerunner), the Kontakion of the Baptist (**"O Prophet of God and Forerunner of grace..."**); on Wednesday and Friday (the Precious Cross), the Kontakion of the Cross (**"O Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross of Thine own will..."**); on Thursday (the Holy Apostles and St. Nicholas), the Kontakion of the Apostles (**"The sure and divinely inspired heralds..."**) and of St. Nicholas (**"In Myra, O Holy One, thou wast proved a true priest..."**); and on Saturday (the Departed and All Saints), the Kontakion of the Departed (**"With the Saints grant rest, O Christ, to the souls of Thy servants..."**) and that of All Saints (**"As first-fruits of our nature to the Planter of creation..."**). On all ordinary ferias, the sequence concludes with **"O Protection of Christians unashamed..."** (*Προστασία τῶν Χριστιανῶν*). Violakis further details the complex intersection when the Annunciation of the Theotokos (March 25) coincides with days of Great Lent, Lazarus Saturday, Palm Sunday, Holy Week, or Pascha (Kyriopascha): on Lazarus Saturday or Palm Sunday, the festal Kontakion of the day is combined with that of the Annunciation (*Τῇ ὑπερμάχῳ*); on Pascha and throughout Bright Week, the Paschal Kontakion (*Εἰ καὶ ἐν τάφῳ*) and the Annunciation Kontakion are coordinated according to the precedence of the Resurrection.
[^105]: The Trisagion and Its Baptismal and Stauropegic Substitutions (Violakis, pp. 100–101, §41). Paragraph 41 codifies the liturgical chants accompanying the Little Entrance before the Scriptural lections (Epistle and Gospel). The normative hymn of the Byzantine Eucharistic liturgy is the Thrice-Holy Hymn (Trisagion): **"Holy God, Holy Mighty, Holy Immortal, have mercy on us"** (*Ἅγιος ὁ Θεός, Ἅγιος Ἰσχυρός, Ἅγιος Ἀθάνατος, ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς*). On ancient baptismal days—the Great Feasts of the Lord where corporate catechumen baptisms were historically performed—the Trisagion is displaced by the Pauline baptismal hymn (Galatians 3:27): **"As many of you as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ: Alleluia"** (*Ὅσοι εἰς Χριστὸν ἐβαπτίσθητε, Χριστὸν ἐνεδύσασθε. Ἀλληλούϊα*). These designated days are: (1) The Nativity of Christ (December 25); (2) The Theophany of the Lord (January 6); (3) Lazarus Saturday; (4) Holy and Great Saturday (at the Vesperal Liturgy of St. Basil the Great); (5) Holy Pascha (Easter Sunday) and every day of Bright Week through Saturday; and (6) The Great Day of Pentecost (Sunday of Holy Pentecost). On feasts dedicated to the Adoration of the Holy Cross—namely the Universal Elevation of the Precious and Life-Giving Cross (September 14) and the Third Sunday of Great Lent (Veneration of the Precious Cross)—the Trisagion is replaced by the hymn of veneration: **"Thy Cross do we adore, O Master, and Thy holy Resurrection do we glorify"** (*Τὸν Σταυρόν σου προσκυνοῦμεν, Δέσποτα, καὶ τὴν ἁγίαν σου Ἀνάστασιν δοξάζομεν*).
[^106]: Precedence and Structure of the Scriptural Lectionary (Violakis, pp. 102–103, §42). Paragraph 42 governs the order, selection, and pairing of the Scriptural Lections at the Divine Liturgy, comprising the Prokimenon, Epistle (*Apostolos*), Alleluia verses, and Gospel (*Evangelion*). Great Dominical Feasts possess absolute lectionary priority: on such days, only the proper readings of the Feast are proclaimed, suppressing both Sunday and weekday lections. On ordinary Sundays, the Sunday Resurrection Prokimenon, Epistle, Alleluia, and Gospel take primacy; when a Feast of the Theotokos, an Afterfeast, or a celebrated Saint coincides with Sunday, a dual reading structure is enacted: the Sunday Epistle is read first, followed by the Epistle of the Saint, preceded by their respective Prokimena (the Sunday Prokimenon intoned first, followed by the Prokimenon of the Saint). The Gospel readings mirror this succession: first the Sunday Gospel lection according to the Matthean or Lucan cycle, followed by the Gospel lection of the Saint. On ordinary weekdays with a celebrated Saint, either both the daily feria lection and the Saint's lection are proclaimed in succession, or the Saint's reading supplants the feria if the commemoration is of higher rubrical rank. On Saturdays, a distinctive dual lectionary structure is maintained, pairing the Saturday cycle with commemorations of martyrs or the departed.
[^107]: Post-Epistolary Alleluia, the Holy Saturday Prokimenon, and 1888 Greek Typographical Galley Defect (Violakis, pp. 104–105, §43). Paragraph 43 regulates the responsorial chant sung between the Epistle and the Gospel. The universal Byzantine norm is the chanting of the Alleluia in the appointed Tone with its scriptural psalm verses. On ordinary Sundays, the Alleluia of the Sunday Tone is intoned with two verses; on Feasts and celebrated Saints, proper festal Alleluia verses are proclaimed. At the Vesperal Liturgy of St. Basil on Holy and Great Saturday, the Alleluia is suppressed; in its place, the Reader intones the great Resurrectional Prokimenon in Tone 7: **"Arise, O God, judge the earth, for Thou shalt inherit among all nations"** (Psalm 81:8 LXX: *Ἀνάστα, ὁ Θεός, κρῖνον τὴν γῆν, ὅτι σὺ κατακληρονομήσεις ἐν πᾶσι τοῖς ἔθνεσι*), during which the clergy exchange their dark Lenten vestments for bright white vestments and scatter laurel leaves and flower petals throughout the church. Textual and Epigraphic Note: In the 1888 editio princeps, an egregious typographical compositor defect occurred across leaves p117–p120 (printed pages 102–105). At the bottom of p. 102 (leaf p117), §43 abruptly broke off mid-sentence: `...εἰς τὸ τέλος ἑκάστου τῶν Ἀποστόλων τῆς` (*"...at the end of each Apostolos of this..."*). At the top of p. 104 (leaf p119), the printer mistakenly inserted a duplicate galley slug from p. 106 (`...τοῦ ἑορταζομένου Ἁγίου μνήμη τῷ Σαββάτῳ τῆς Ἀποδόσεως...`). Fortunately, the facing English translation on p. 105 (leaf p120) preserves the genuine conclusion of §43: *"together with its verses — and the Choirs are obliged to sing it."* The original Greek is restored herein from parallel Byzantine liturgical rubrics: `[ἑβδομάδος ταύτης εὕρηται γεγραμμένον τὸ Ἀλληλούϊα τρὶς μετὰ τῶν στίχων αὐτοῦ, καὶ ὀφείλουσιν οἱ Χοροὶ ψάλλειν αὐτό]`, resolving this historical printing error.
[^108]: The Cherubic Hymn and Its Seasonal Holy Week Substitutions (Violakis, pp. 104–105, §44). Paragraph 44 governs the Cherubic Hymn (*Χερουβικὸς Ὕμνος*) intoned during the Great Entrance with the Holy Gifts at the Divine Liturgy. The universal norm of the Byzantine Rite is the classic hymn in the Tone of the day (or chosen melody): **"We who mystically represent the Cherubim, and sing the Thrice-Holy Hymn to the Life-Giving Trinity, let us now lay aside all earthly care. That we may receive the King of all, escorted invisibly by ranks of angels: Alleluia, alleluia, alleluia"** (*Οἱ τὰ Χερουβεὶμ μυστικῶς εἰκονίζοντες...*). On Holy and Great Thursday, the Cherubic Hymn is displaced by the Eucharistic troparion in Tone 6: **"Of Thy Mystical Supper, O Son of God, receive me today as a communicant; for I will not speak of Thy Mystery to Thine enemies, neither will I give Thee a kiss as did Judas, but like the thief will I confess Thee: Remember me, O Lord, when Thou comest into Thy Kingdom"** (*Τοῦ Δείπνου σου τοῦ μυστικοῦ σήμερον, Υἱὲ Θεοῦ, κοινωνόν με παράλαβε...*). On Holy and Great Saturday, at the Liturgy of St. Basil, the Cherubic Hymn is replaced by the ancient solemn chant from the Liturgy of St. James in Tone 5: **"Let all mortal flesh keep silence, and stand with fear and trembling, and ponder nothing earthly within itself; for the King of kings and Lord of lords cometh forth to be slain, and given as food to the faithful. And the ranks of angels go before Him with every principality and power, the many-eyed Cherubim and six-winged Seraphim, covering their faces and crying out the hymn: Alleluia, alleluia, alleluia"** (*Σιγησάτω πᾶσα σὰρξ βροτεία...*). At the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, the Cherubic Hymn is replaced by: **"Now the Powers of heaven minister invisibly with us; for behold, the King of glory enters; behold, the mystical sacrifice, fully accomplished, is ushered in. In faith and love let us draw near, that we may become partakers of eternal life: Alleluia, alleluia, alleluia"** (*Νῦν αἱ Δυνάμεις τῶν οὐρανῶν...*).
[^109]: The Megalynarion and Hirmos of Ode 9 (*Axion Estin*) (Violakis, pp. 104–105, §45). Paragraph 45 defines the Eucharistic Hymn to the Mother of God (*Megalynarion* or *Hirmos of the Ninth Ode*) chanted immediately following the Epiclesis and Eucharistic consecration at the priest's exclamation: **"Especially for our most holy, pure, blessed and glorious Lady, the Theotokos and Ever-Virgin Mary"**. In the Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom, the standard hymn is the ancient Athonite chant: **"It is truly right to bless thee, O Theotokos, ever-blessed and most blameless and the Mother of our God. More honorable than the Cherubim, and more glorious beyond compare than the Seraphim, who without corruption gavest birth to God the Word, true Theotokos, thee do we magnify"** (*Ἄξιόν ἐστιν ὡς ἀληθῶς μακαρίζειν σε τὴν Θεοτόκον...*). In the Divine Liturgy of St. Basil the Great, the Megalynarion is the hymn attributed to St. John of Damascus in Tone 8: **"All of creation rejoices in thee, O full of grace: the angelic assembly and the human race; hallowed temple and rational paradise, glory of virgins, of whom God took flesh and became a child, He Who is our God before the ages. For He made thy womb a throne, and shaped thy body broader than the heavens. All of creation rejoices in thee, O full of grace: glory to thee!"** (*Ἐπὶ σοὶ χαίρει, Κεχαριτωμένη, πᾶσα ἡ κτίσις...*). On Dominical and Marian Feasts (and throughout their Afterfeasts and Apodoses), both *Axion Estin* and *Epi Soi Chairei* are replaced by the Zadostoynyk (*Ἀντὶ τοῦ Ἀξιόν ἐστιν*), consisting of the Megalynarion refrain and the Hirmos of the Ninth Ode of the festal Canon (e.g., at Pascha: **"The angel cried unto the One full of grace: Pure Virgin, rejoice! And again I say: Rejoice! Thy Son hath risen from the grave on the third day..."** followed by **"Shine, shine, O New Jerusalem, for the glory of the Lord is risen upon thee..."**; and similarly on the Annunciation, Nativity, Theophany, Ascension, Pentecost, etc.).
[^110]: The Byzantine Eucharistic Communion Hymn System, Weekly and Festal Lections, and LXX Versification (Violakis, pp. 106–109, §46). Paragraph 46 codifies the comprehensive cyclical and hierarchy of the Eucharistic Communion Hymn (*Κοινωνικόν*, Slavonic *Prichasten*) chanted during the communion of the clergy and the faithful at the Divine Liturgy. In classical Byzantine euchology, the Communion Hymn represents an ancient responsorial psalmody whose scriptural verses accompany the distribution of the Holy Mysteries, culminating in the triple Alleluia. Violakis delineates four primary lectionary tiers: (1) Sunday Ordinary: The universal Dominical Communion Hymn is Psalm 148:1 LXX: **"Praise the Lord from the heavens, praise Him in the highest: Alleluia"** (*Αἰνεῖτε τὸν Κύριον ἐκ τῶν οὐρανῶν, αἰνεῖτε αὐτὸν ἐν τοῖς ὑψίστοις. Ἀλληλούϊα*), appointed on all ordinary Sundays, on the Feast of the Circumcision (January 1), and on the Vesperal Liturgies of Christmas and Theophany Eve conjoined with St. Basil. (2) Dominical and Marian Feasts: Great Feasts of the Lord suppress the Sunday lection in favor of proper Dominical verses (e.g. Pascha: **"Receive the Body of Christ; taste of the Fountain of immortality"**; Ascension: Psalm 46:6 LXX; Pentecost: Psalm 142:10 LXX; Exaltation: Psalm 4:7 LXX). For Feasts of the Theotokos and the Indiction (September 1), the standard Communion Hymn is Psalm 115:4 LXX: **"I will take the cup of salvation, and call upon the Name of the Lord: Alleluia"** (*Ποτήριον σωτηρίου λήψομαι*); on the Annunciation (March 25), this is replaced by Psalm 131:13 LXX: **"The Lord hath chosen Zion; He hath desired it for His habitation: Alleluia"** (*Ἐξελέξατο Κύριος τὴν Σιών*). (3) Celebrated Saints on Sunday: When major commemorations coincide with Sunday, proper verses replace *Aineite*: Forerunner: Psalm 111:6b LXX (**"The righteous shall be in everlasting remembrance"**); Apostles, Evangelists, and Hierarchs: Psalm 18:5 LXX (**"Their sound is gone out into all the earth..."**); Archangels (November 8): Psalm 103:4 LXX (**"Who maketh His angels spirits..."**); Mid-Lent and August 1: Psalm 4:7 LXX (**"The light of Thy countenance hath been signed upon us, O Lord"**); the four Sundays after Thomas: **"Receive the Body of Christ"**; All Saints: Psalm 32:1 LXX (**"Rejoice in the Lord, O ye righteous"**). (4) Weekday Feria Cycle: Monday (Angels, Ps. 103:4 LXX); Tuesday (Forerunner, Ps. 111:6b LXX); Wednesday (Theotokos, Ps. 115:4 LXX); Thursday (Apostles, Ps. 18:5 LXX); Friday (Holy Cross, Ps. 73:12 LXX: **"Thou hast wrought salvation in the midst of the earth, O Christ God"**); Saturday (Departed and All Saints, Ps. 64:5 LXX: **"Blessed are they whom Thou hast chosen and taken to Thyself, O Lord..."**). At the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, the proper Communion chant is Psalm 33:9 LXX: **"O taste and see that the Lord is good: Alleluia"** (*Γεύσασθε καὶ ἴδετε ὅτι χρηστὸς ὁ Κύριος*), unless displaced by a celebrated Saint's verse.
[^111]: Post-Communion Hymnody (*Eidomen to Phos*) and Dominical Substitutions (Violakis, pp. 108–109, §47). Paragraph 47 defines the post-communion hymnody executed immediately after the faithful have received the Holy Mysteries, following the Deacon's invitation **"With fear of God, with faith and with love draw near"** (*Μετὰ φόβου Θεοῦ, πίστεως καὶ ἀγάπης προσέλθετε*) and the Priest's solemn blessing over the congregation: **"O God, save Thy people, and bless Thine inheritance"** (*Σῶσον, ὁ Θεός, τὸν λαόν σου, καὶ εὐλόγησον τὴν κληρονομίαν σου*). The normative hymn of the Byzantine Eucharistic liturgies of St. John Chrysostom and St. Basil the Great is the ancient Pentecostarion troparion in Tone 2: **"We have seen the true Light, we have received the Heavenly Spirit, we have found the true Faith, worshipping the Undivided Trinity: for He hath saved us"** (*Εἴδομεν τὸ φῶς τὸ ἀληθινόν, ἐλάβομεν Πνεῦμα ἐπουράνιον, εὕρομεν πίστιν ἀληθῆ, ἀδιαίρετον Τριάδα προσκυνοῦντες· αὕτη γὰρ ἡμᾶς ἔσωσεν*). Violakis establishes the liturgical displacements of this hymn across the liturgical year: (1) On Dominical Feasts and their Apodoses, on the Third Sunday of Great Lent (Veneration of the Cross), and on Lazarus Saturday, the Dismissal Troparion of the Feast is chanted instead. (2) On Holy and Great Thursday, the choir chants recitatively (*chyma*) the Troparion of the Mystical Supper: **"Of Thy Mystical Supper, O Son of God, accept me today as a communicant..."** (*Τοῦ δείπνου σου τοῦ μυστικοῦ*). (3) On Holy and Great Saturday, the choir chants in Tone 2 irmologically the Troparion of the Penitent Thief: **"Remember us also, O Compassionate One, as Thou didst remember the thief in the Kingdom of Heaven"** (*Μνήσθητι εὔσπλαγχνε καὶ ἡμῶν καθὼς ἐμνημόνευσας τοῦ λῃστοῦ ἐν τῇ βασιλείᾳ τῶν οὐρανῶν*). (4) Throughout Paschaltide (from Easter Sunday until its Apodosis), the Paschal Troparion **"Christ is risen from the dead..."** (*Χριστὸς ἀνέστη*) is sung daily. (5) From Ascension until its Apodosis, the festal Troparion **"Thou hast ascended in glory, O Christ our God..."** (*Ἀνελήφθης ἐν δόξῃ*) is sung. (6) On the Saturday before Pentecost (Soul Saturday), the troparion of the departed **"O Thou Who by the depth of wisdom..."** (*Ὁ βάθει σοφίας*) is sung. (7) On Pentecost Sunday, the Troparion **"Blessed art Thou, O Christ our God..."** (*Εὐλογητὸς εἶ Χριστέ*); *Eidomen to Phos* resumes on Monday of the Holy Spirit. (8) At the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, *Eidomen to Phos* is never sung; the Reader or choir recites Psalm 33:2 LXX: **"I will bless the Lord at all times; His praise shall continually be in my mouth"** (*Εὐλογήσω τὸν Κύριον ἐν παντὶ καιρῷ*).
[^112]: The Post-Ambo Acclamation *Eiē to Onoma Kyriou* and Bright Week Displacement (Violakis, pp. 108–111, §48). Paragraph 48 governs the responsorial acclamation following the Prayer behind the Ambo (*Ὀπισθάμβωνος Εὐχή*): **"Blessed be the Name of the Lord, from this time forth and unto the ages"** (Psalm 112:2 LXX: *Εἴη τὸ ὄνομα Κυρίου εὐλογημένον ἀπὸ τοῦ νῦν καὶ ἕως τοῦ αἰῶνος*). In all three Byzantine Eucharistic Liturgies (Chrysostom, Basil, and Presanctified), this scriptural verse is chanted thrice in Tone 2 in recitative style (*chyma*). During Bright Week (from Easter Sunday through Bright Saturday) and on the Apodosis of Pascha, this scriptural acclamation is suppressed and replaced by the triple singing of the Paschal Troparion **"Christ is risen from the dead, trampling down death by death, and upon those in the tombs bestowing life"** (*Χριστὸς ἀνέστη*), likewise executed in Tone 2 *chyma*.
[^113]: Sacerdotal Benediction (*Eulogia Kyriou*) and Artoklasia Conjunction (Violakis, pp. 110–111, §49). Paragraph 49 highlights the solemn sacerdotal blessing imparted immediately before the Dismissal of the Divine Liturgy: **"The blessing of the Lord and His mercy come upon you, through His grace and love for mankind, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages"** (*Εὐλογία Κυρίου καὶ ἔλεος ἔλθοι ἐφ’ ἡμᾶς, τῇ αὐτοῦ χάριτι καὶ φιλανθρωπίᾳ πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων*). Violakis notes that this same solemn apostolic benediction concludes the rite of the Blessing of the Loaves (*Artoklasia*) at Great Vespers. The text is derived from ancient Byzantine euchological formulas connecting priestly intercession with divine beneficence. Note on the Father Paul Doxology Standard: The Greek original concludes with the traditional liturgical doxology *πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων*, which in the English facing text was translated loosely as "now, and ever, and unto ages of ages"; under MTS-1, this is standardized to the canonical UGCC form: *"always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages."*
[^114]: The Universal Liturgical Seal (*Di' Euchōn*) and Hierarchical Acclamation (Violakis, pp. 110–111, §50). Paragraph 50 treats the final liturgical seal (*ἐπισφράγισμα*) of Byzantine liturgical prayer: **"Through the prayers of our holy Fathers, Lord Jesus Christ our God, have mercy on us"** (*Δι’ εὐχῶν τῶν ἁγίων Πατέρων ἡμῶν, Κύριε Ἰησοῦ Χριστὲ ὁ Θεός, ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς*). This formula concludes every liturgical office, private prayer rule, and Eucharistic Liturgy in the Byzantine tradition. Violakis details two critical rubrical distinctions: (1) Bright Week Paschal Variant: Throughout Bright Week, *Di' euchōn* is suppressed at both Vespers and the Liturgy. The Priest instead proclaims the triple Paschal greeting: **"Christ is risen!"** (*Χριστὸς ἀνέστη!*), the faithful responding: **"Truly He is risen!"** (*Ἀληθῶς ἀνέστη!*). The Priest then exclaims: **"Glory to His third-day Resurrection!"** (*Δόξα τῇ αὐτοῦ τριημέρῳ ἐγέρσει!*), the people answering: **"We worship His third-day Resurrection!"** (*Προσκυνοῦμεν αὐτοῦ τὴν τριήμερον ἔγερσιν!*), followed by the complete chanting of *Christos anesti*. (2) Hierarchical Rubric: Whenever an Archbishop, Metropolitan, or Patriarch presides at the service, the formula changes from the plural *holy Fathers* (*ἁγίων Πατέρων*) to the singular hierarchical invocation: **"Through the prayers of our holy Master [Bishop], Lord Jesus Christ our God, have mercy on us. Amen"** (*Δι’ εὐχῶν τοῦ ἁγίου Δεσπότου ἡμῶν, Κύριε Ἰησοῦ Χριστὲ ὁ Θεός, ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς. Ἀμήν*), acknowledging the presiding archpastor as the apostolic icon of Christ within the synaxis.
[^115]: Typikon of Weekday Daily Vespers and the Four Combination Rules (Violakis, pp. 112–115, §1 & Note 1). This section opens the second major division of the 1888 Violakis Typikon: the Order of Daily Services throughout the year (*Τυπικὴ Διάταξις τῆς καθ' ἡμέραν Ἀκολουθίας*). Paragraph 1 codifies the standard structure of weekday Vespers outside festal periods: (1) The Ninth Hour: Footnote 1 notes that the Ninth Hour precedes Vespers, referring back to Pre-theory §1 where its conjunction with Vespers was established. (2) Structure at *Kyrie ekekraxa*: A total of six Stichira: two from the first group of three in the Parakletike (Tone of the week), one from the second group of three in the Parakletike, and three of the commemorated Saint from the Menaion. Doxastikon / Theotokion / Stavrotheotokion (on Tuesday/Thursday evening). (3) The Four Rules (α΄–δ΄): Rule α΄: Saint with 6 stichira in Menaion without Doxastikon: 3 stichira of the Saint are omitted, and 3 stichira of the Parakletike precede the remaining 3 of the Saint. Rule β΄: Saint with 2 Doxastika: only the Saint's stichira are sung (each repeated twice to make 6), completely omitting the Parakletike. Rule γ΄: Two Saints commemorated with proper stichira: each Saint receives 3 stichira, omitting the Parakletike. Rule δ΄: Friday evening Vespers for Saturday: If the Saturday Saint has no Doxastikon, at *Doxa Kai nyn* we chant the First Dogmatic Theotokion of the Tone of the week. If the Saint has a Doxastikon, *Doxa* of the Saint and *Kai nyn* the First Dogmatic Theotokion. If the Saint has no Doxastikon, the Dismissal Troparion is *Apostles, Martyrs, and Prophets*, *Doxa: Remember O Lord*, *Kai nyn: Holy Mother*. (4) Vesting and Sanctuary Movement: At weekday Vespers, no Little Entrance is made (unless it is an Apodosis of a Dominical/Marian Feast). The Royal Doors remain closed throughout. The priest vests only in the epitrachilion, enters through the south deacon's door into the Sanctuary, and exits through the north door to cense during the chanting of *Kateuthyntheto*.
[^116]: Daily Midnight Office Conjunction with Matins and the Solemn Fortyfold Kyrie Intercession (Violakis, pp. 114–115, §2). Paragraph 2 governs the commencement of weekday Orthros and its seamless conjunction with the Midnight Office (*Mesonyktikon*): (1) Initial Enarxis: The Priest gives the opening blessing **"Blessed is our God..."**, followed by **"Glory to Thee, our God..."**, **"O Heavenly King..."**, the Trisagion Prayers, the ecphonesis **"For Thine is the Kingdom..."**, **"Lord, have mercy"** (twelve times), the Doxology (*Doxa, Kai nyn*), the triple invitation to worship (**"O come, let us worship..."**), Psalm 50 LXX, and the Midnight Office according to the Horologion. (2) Forefeast / Afterfeast Adaptations: On days of Forefeasts and Afterfeasts, the nocturnal Troparion **"Behold, the Bridegroom cometh at midnight"** (*Ἰδοὺ ὁ Νυμφίος*) is replaced by the Troparion of the Forefeast or Feast, and the concluding Troparia (*Mnesthēti Kyrie*) are replaced by the festal Kontakia. (3) Penitential Troparia and Temple Action: Immediately following the Mesonyktikon, the choir chants the Penitential Troparia (*Eleēson hēmas Kyrie*, *Doxa: Kyrie eleēson hēmas*, *Kai nyn: Tēs eusplanchnias tēn pylēn*). During the chanting of *Tēs eusplanchnias*, the curtain (*parapetasma*) behind the Royal Doors is drawn aside. The priest, vested in his epitrachilion, emerges from the Sanctuary, stands before the closed Holy Doors facing east, and intones the Great Litany of Supplication (*Ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς ὁ Θεός...*). (4) The Fortyfold Kyrie Eleison: At the petition for preservation from wrath, famine, pestilence, earthquake, flood, and foreign invasion, both choirs alternate in chanting **"Lord, have mercy"** in four sets of ten (forty times in total: 10 by First Choir, 10 by Second Choir, 10 by First Choir, 10 by Second Choir), reflecting the ancient penitential monastic discipline of the Byzantine Horologion.
[^117]: The Conclusion of the Daily Midnight Office (*Mesonyktikon*), the Litany of Peace, Mutual Forgiveness, and the Royal Enarxis of Matins (Violakis, pp. 116–117, §2 cont.). This section details the ritual bridge connecting the nocturnal Midnight Office (*Μεσονυκτικόν*) to the morning service of Matins (*Ὄρθρος*). In classical Byzantine horological practice, the Midnight Office culminates in the solemn penitential Litany of Supplication (*Ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς ὁ Θεός...*), followed by the sacerdotal prayer: **"Hear us, O God our Savior, the hope of all the ends of the earth..."** (*Ἐπάκουσον ἡμῶν ὁ Θεὸς ὁ Σωτὴρ ἡμῶν*), and the preliminary Dismissal. The Priest then descends one step from the Sanctuary (*Bema*) toward the solea to intone the ancient Litany for Peace: **"Let us pray for the peace of the world"** (*Εὐξώμεθα ὑπὲρ εἰρήνης τοῦ κόσμου*), during which the choirs chant **"Lord, have mercy"** continuously without pause through all petitions. Upon concluding with the triple Kyrie eleison (*"Let us also say for ourselves: Lord, have mercy [thrice]"*) and the universal seal *Di' euchōn*, the clergy and people exchange the ancient mutual Rite of Forgiveness (*συγχώρησις*, Slavonic *Proshchenie*). Having received mutual absolution from the community, the Priest re-enters the Sanctuary, takes his station before the Holy Table, and inaugurates the Royal Beginning (*Βασιλικὴ Ἔναρξις*) of Matins: **"Blessed is our God, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages"**, followed by the Trisagion Prayers, the Royal Troparia (*Σῶσον Κύριε*, *Ὁ ὑψωθεὶς ἐν τῷ Σταυρῷ*, *Προστασία φοβερά*), and the Royal Litany. Following the choir's response **"In the Name of the Lord, Father, bless"** (*Ἐν ὀνόματι Κυρίου εὐλόγησον, Πάτερ*), the Priest intones the great Trinitarian doxology: **"Glory to the Holy, consubstantial, life-creating, and undivided Trinity..."** (*Δόξα τῇ Ἁγίᾳ καὶ ὁμοουσίῳ καὶ ζωοποιῷ καὶ ἀδιαιρέτῳ Τριάδι...*), which directly introduces the Six Psalms (*Ἑξάψαλμος*, Psalms 3, 37, 62, 87, 102, 142 LXX).
[^118]: Weekday Matins Canon Scheme, Scriptural Refrains (*Stichologia*), and the Magnificat (Violakis, pp. 116–119, §2 cont.). This paragraph delineates the complete hymnographic progression of weekday Matins outside festal periods. Following the Psalter kathisma of the day and Psalm 50 LXX, the Canons from the Parakletike and the Menaion are chanted with specific scriptural and intercessory refrains (*stichoi*): (1) For the penitential troparia (*Katanyktika*) of the primary Canon: **"Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee"** (*Δόξα σοι ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, δόξα σοι*); (2) For the martyric troparia (*Martyrika*): **"Holy martyrs of God, intercede for us"** (*Ἅγιοι τοῦ Θεοῦ πρεσβεύσατε ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν*); (3) For the second Canon of the Parakletike, according to the weekday angelic and saintly dedication: Monday (Archangels: *Ἀρχάγγελοι τοῦ Θεοῦ*), Tuesday (the Forerunner: *Προφῆτα τοῦ Χριστοῦ*), Wednesday and Friday (the Precious Cross: *Ὑπεραγία Θεοτόκε σῶσον ἡμᾶς*), Thursday (Apostles and St. Nicholas: *Ἀπόστολοι τοῦ Χριστοῦ* and *Ἅγιε τοῦ Θεοῦ*), and Saturday (Martyrs and the Departed, utilizing the psalm verses Psalm 24:13 LXX: *Αἱ ψυχαὶ αὐτῶν ἐν ἀγαθοῖς αὐλισθήσονται* and Psalm 64:5 LXX: *Μακάριοι οὓς ἐξελέξω καὶ προσελάβου, Κύριε*); (4) For the Canon of the Saint from the Menaion: **"Holy saint of God, intercede for us"** (*Ἅγιε τοῦ Θεοῦ πρέσβευε ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν*). Structural landmarks within the Canon include: the Heirmos and Kathisma of the Saint after Ode 3; the Heirmos, Kontakion of the day, and Menologion [Synaxarion] after Ode 6; the biblical verse **"We praise, we bless, and we worship the Lord"** (*Αἰνοῦμεν, εὐλογοῦμεν καὶ προσκυνοῦμεν τὸν Κύριον*) before the Heirmos of Ode 8; the Magnificat (**"More honorable than the Cherubim"**, *Τὴν τιμιωτέραν*) with the biblical Song of the Theotokos (Luke 1:46–55); and the Heirmos of Ode 9 from the Menaion. Following the weekday Exapostilaria, the service moves into the Lesser Doxology (*Σοὶ δόξα πρέπει*), the Morning Litany of Supplication (*Πληρώσωμεν τὴν ἑωθινήν*), the Parakletike Aposticha with weekday psalm verses (Psalm 89:14 and 89:17 LXX), **"It is good to give praise unto the Lord"** (Psalm 91:2 LXX), the Trisagion Prayers, the Dismissal Theotokion, and the immediate transition into the Divine Liturgy.
[^119]: The Daily Antiphon System of the Byzantine Divine Liturgy and Scriptural Versification (Violakis, pp. 118–121, §3). Paragraph 3 establishes the authoritative Constantinopolitan standard for the three Daily Antiphons (*καθημερινὰ Ἀντίφωνα*) of the Divine Liturgy on ordinary weekdays throughout the ecclesiastical year: (1) Antiphon One is drawn from Psalm 91 LXX (*Ἀγαθὸν τὸ ἐξομολογεῖσθαι*, Psalm 92 MT), celebrating divine benevolence and constant faithfulness, paired with the universal Marian refrain: **"Through the intercessions of the Theotokos, Savior, save us!"** (*Ταῖς πρεσβείαις τῆς Θεοτόκου, Σῶτερ, σῶσον ἡμᾶς*). (2) Antiphon Two is drawn from Psalm 92 LXX (*Ὁ Κύριος ἐβασίλευσεν*, Psalm 93 MT), celebrating the cosmic sovereignty of God and the beauty of His sanctuary, paired with the refrain: **"Through the prayers of Thy Saints, save us, O Lord!"** (*Πρεσβείαις τῶν Ἁγίων σου, σῶσον ἡμᾶς, Κύριε*), and concluding with the 6th-century Christological hymn of Emperor Justinian: **"O Only-begotten Son and Word of God"** (*Ὁ μονογενὴς Υἱὸς καὶ Λόγος τοῦ Θεοῦ*). (3) Antiphon Three is drawn from Psalm 94 LXX (*Δεῦτε ἀγαλλιασώμεθα*, Psalm 95 MT), the great invitatory psalm of adoration, paired with the weekday refrain: **"Save us, O Son of God, Who art wonderful in Thy Saints, who sing to Thee: Alleluia!"** (*Σῶσον ἡμᾶς Υἱὲ Θεοῦ, ὁ ἐν Ἁγίοις θαυμαστός, ψάλλοντάς σοι Ἀλληλούϊα*). At the Little Entrance, this refrain directly provides the text for the Entrance Hymn (*Εἰσοδικόν*): **"O come, let us worship and fall down before Christ! Save us, O Son of God, Who art wonderful in Thy Saints..."** Note on Septuagint versification: Under MTS-1, all psalm citations strictly reflect Septuagint (LXX) chapter and verse numbering, avoiding the Masoretic divergence found in modern Western printings (e.g. RSV, KJV).
[^120]: The Weekday Kontakion Rotation and the Universal Marian Alternative (*Prostasia*) (Violakis, pp. 122–123, §3 & Note 2). This rubric governs the post-Entrance dismissal hymnody at the weekday Divine Liturgy. Following the Little Entrance, the choir chants: (1) The Dismissal Troparion of the Saint of the day; (2) The Dismissal Troparion of the Patron Saint of the Church temple; and (3) The Kontakion of the day of the week, following the classical Byzantine weekly cycle: Monday (Bodiless Powers: *Ἀρχιστράτηγοι Θεοῦ*, Tone 2); Tuesday (the Forerunner: *Προφῆτα Θεοῦ καὶ πρόδρομε*, Tone 2); Wednesday and Friday (the Precious Cross: *Ὁ ὑψωθεὶς ἐν τῷ Σταυρῷ*, Tone 4); Thursday (the Holy Apostles: *Τοὺς ἀσφαλεῖς καὶ θεοφθόγγους*, Tone 2); Saturday (All Saints and the Faithful Departed: *Ὡς ἀπαρχὰς τῆς φύσεως*, Tone 8). In Note 2, Violakis records an important customary divergence prevalent in 19th-century Greek parish practice: *"Some chant on every day the Kontakion: Protection of Christians"* (*Τινὲς ψάλλουσι καθ' ἑκάστην Κοντάκιον Προστασία τῶν Χριστιανῶν*, Tone 2: **"O Protection of Christians unashamed, Mediatrix before the Creator unfailing..."**). While monastic typika preserve the strict weekly rota of patristic Kontakia, modern parish use frequently substitutes *Prostasia tōn Christianōn* as a daily general Marian Kontakion on weekdays lacking a proper festal Kontakion.
[^121]: The Typika and First Hour Synthesis when the Divine Liturgy is Omitted (Violakis, pp. 122–123, §3 cont.). Paragraph 3 outlines the liturgical adaptation required whenever the Divine Liturgy does not follow weekday Matins—a common occurrence in non-monastic parish churches where daily Eucharistic celebration was not customary. Instead of an abrupt dismissal, the service seamlessly integrates the First Hour (*Α΄ Ὥρα*) and elements of the Reader's Typika (*Τυπικά*, Slavonic *Izobrazitel'ny*): immediately after the reading of **"It is good to give praise unto the Lord"** (*Ἀγαθὸν τὸ ἐξομολογεῖσθαι*), the Trisagion Prayers are recited, followed by the Dismissal Troparion of the Saint; the First Hour is conjoined directly via the triple invitatory **"O come, let us worship..."**; after the First Hour, the liturgical scriptural lections appointed for the day—the Epistle [Apostolos] and Gospel—are proclaimed; the Priest then intones the Litany of Supplication (*Ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς ὁ Θεός*), the ecphonesis, the ancient dialogue **"Wisdom! ... He Who is, blessed is Christ our God..."**, the choir's petition **"Establish, O Lord God, the holy Orthodox Faith..."**, and the Priest's Great Dismissal with *Di' euchōn*. This ensures that the biblical and liturgical cycle of the daily lectionary is proclaimed even in the absence of the Eucharistic synaxis.
[^122]: Typikon of Forefeasts (*Proeortia*) and Afterfeasts (*Metheorta*) with a Celebrated Saint: Vesperal and Matins Synthesis (Violakis, pp. 122–125, §4). Paragraph 4 codifies the precise combinatory mechanics applied when a day of preparation (*Προεόρτιον*) or afterfeast (*Μεθέορτον*) of a Dominical or Marian Feast coincides with the commemoration of a Saint possessing proper Doxastika (*ἔχων Δοξαστικά*) in the Menaion. At Vespers, six Stichira are chanted at *Lord, I have cried*: three of the season (Forefeast or Afterfeast) and three of the Saint; the Saint's hymnographic dignity is prioritized at *Doxa*, while the festal season is retained at *Kai nyn*. The same balanced synthesis occurs at the Aposticha. A crucial canonical distinction is codified regarding the sacerdotal Dismissal (*Ἀπόλυσις*): On Afterfeast days, the Priest must pronounce the distinctive festal dismissal incipit characteristic of the feast (e.g. at the Transfiguration: **"May He Who was transfigured in glory before His holy disciples and apostles upon Mount Tabor, Christ our true God..."**); on Forefeast days, however, the Priest is strictly forbidden from using the festal incipit, retaining instead the standard daily dismissal formula, because the historical saving event commemorated by the feast has not yet arrived in liturgical time.
[^123]: Eucharistic Antiphons and Entrance Refrains during Afterfeasts: Festal vs. Daily Antiphon Practice (Violakis, pp. 124–125, §5 & Note 3). Paragraph 5 and Footnote 3 address the Eucharistic psalmody and sanctuary movement on Afterfeasts of Dominical and Marian Feasts. Violakis prescribes that on days of Afterfeasts, the Divine Liturgy appoints the proper festal Antiphons of the feast with their respective refrains, and the Little Entrance hymn (*Eisodikon*) incorporates the feast's characteristic refrain (e.g. **"Save us, O Son of God, Who wast baptized in the Jordan by John..."** or **"Who wast transfigured on Mount Tabor..."**). Footnote 3 documents an authentic liturgical variant: *"Some chant the daily Antiphons even on Afterfeast days"* (*Τινὲς καὶ εἰς τὰς μεθεόρτους ἡμέρας ψάλλουσι τὰ καθημερινὰ Ἀντίφωνα*). In earlier monastic typika (and throughout the traditional Ruthenian and Slavic recensions), festal antiphons are strictly reserved for the day of the Feast itself and its Apodosis, while ordinary afterfeast weekdays employ either the Daily Antiphons (Psalms 91, 92, 94 LXX) or the Beatitudes / Typika with troparia from the Canons. Violakis records both practices, demonstrating the coexistence of the older monastic use with the 19th-century Constantinopolitan parochial simplification.
[^124]: Sacerdotal Ecphonesis, the Voice of the Assembly, and the Choral Amen (Violakis, pp. 126–127, §1 & Note 1). This rubric and Note 1 underscore the fundamental dialogical structure of Byzantine liturgical prayer. In Note 1, Violakis emphasizes: *"At every ecphonesis of the Priest concluding with 'Amen,' the choir is obligated, as representing the people, to chant 'Amen' with melody and piety; otherwise it violates its duty as chanter"* (*Ἐν ἑκάστῃ Ἐκφωνήσει τοῦ Ἱερέως καταληγούσῃ εἰς τὸ Ἀμήν, ὁ χορὸς ὀφείλει ἐκ προσώπου τοῦ λαοῦ λέγειν μετὰ μέλους καὶ εὐλαβείας τό Ἀμήν· ἄλλως παραβαίνει καθῆκον αὑτοῦ ὡς ψάλτου*). In ancient Christian ecclesiology, the liturgical *Amen* (Hebrew: "so be it", "truly") is not a passive musical coda, but the indispensable corporate ratification by the assembly of the royal priesthood, sealing the sacerdotal prayers (*ekphōnēseis*) offered at the Holy Table. As St. Justin Martyr witnessed in the second century (First Apology 65, 67) and St. John Chrysostom affirmed in the fourth century (Homilies on Second Corinthians 18), the entire congregation seals the Eucharistic and horological prayers through this single vocal assent. When the chanter or choir answers on behalf of the faithful (*ek prosōpou tou laou*), the response must be executed with attentiveness, reverence, and musical decorum, never clipped, swallowed, or omitted.
[^125]: Sunday Vesperal Psalmody, Stichologia Inviolability, and the Lucernarium Structure (Violakis, pp. 128–129, §1 cont.). Paragraph 1 establishes the canonical order of Saturday evening Vespers inaugurating the Lord's Day (*Kyriakē*). The service commences with the Introductory Psalm (Psalm 103 LXX, *Bless the Lord, O my soul*), followed by the Great Litany and the full First Kathisma of the Psalter (Psalms 1–8 LXX, *Blessed is the man*). The Lucernarium (*Λυχνικόν*, the Lamplighting Psalms: Psalms 140, 141, 129, and 116 LXX) is chanted on ten verses, distributing seven Resurrection Stichira from the Octoechos and three Prosomoia of the occurring Saint from the Menaion (or six of the Resurrection and four of the Saint if a Doxastikon is appointed). Violakis inserts a sharp rubrical injunction: *"It is to be noted that under no circumstances is it permitted for the chanters to omit or swallow words from the above verses which are chanted before the Stichira, because they actually sin; but each chanter is obliged to chant these verses in their entirety, audibly for all the faithful in church."* This reflects a systemic pastoral struggle against the hurried abbreviation of the biblical Psalter in late 19th-century urban parishes. The psalmody culminates in the solemn Vesperal Entrance (*Eisodos*) with the ancient third-century hymn *Phos Hilaron* (**"O Gladsome Light"**), followed by the Saturday Great Prokeimenon: **"The Lord reigns, He is robed in majesty"** (*Ὁ Κύριος ἐβασίλευσεν*, Psalm 92 LXX) chanted thrice in Tone 2 with its biblical verses, and the four Resurrection Aposticha Stichira beginning antiphonally from the Second Choir.
[^126]: The Sunday Midnight Office (*Mesonyktikon*), the Triadic Canons, and Megalynaria (Violakis, pp. 130–131, §2). Paragraph 2 delineates the distinctive theological architecture of the Sunday Midnight Office (*Μεσονυκτικόν*). Unlike the weekday Midnight Office—which centers upon the 17th Kathisma (Psalm 118 LXX, the *Amomos*) and solemn prayers for the departed—the Sunday nocturnal office is explicitly Trinitarian and Paschal in character. Because Sunday celebrates the Lord's Resurrection, the penitential Kathisma is omitted and replaced by the Triadic Canon (*Τριαδικὸς Κανών*) of the Octoechos in the Tone of the week, composed by St. Metrophanes of Smyrna (ninth century). If the Saint of the day possesses a Liti, the Liti stichira are appended to the Canon; otherwise, the choir immediately chants the four solemn Triadic Megalynaria: (1) **"It is truly meet to hymn the transcendent Trinity"** (*Ἄξιόν ἐστιν ὡς ἀληθῶς τὴν ὑπέρθεον ὑμνεῖν Τριάδα*); (2) **"It is truly meet to glorify Thee, O God the Word"** (*Ἄξιόν ἐστιν ὡς ἀληθῶς τοῦ δοξάζειν σε τὸν Θεὸν Λόγον*); (3) **"Let us all hymn in godlike manner with inspired songs"** (*Ὑμνήσωμεν πάντες θεοπρεπῶς ᾄσμασιν ἐνθέοις*); and (4) the Marian Theotokion: **"Beholding Thy Son risen from the dead, O pure Virgin"** (*Ἐκ νεκρῶν ἰδοῦσα τὸν σὸν Υἱόν, Ἄχραντε Παρθένε*). Following the Trisagion Prayers, the Hypakoe of the Tone (or the Dismissal Troparion of a celebrated Saint) is chanted, leading into the litany of supplication and the preliminary dismissal before Matins.
[^127]: Sunday Matins Enarxis, Patristic Injunctions on Silence, and Psalter Stichologia (Violakis, pp. 132–133, §2 cont.). This section codifies the beginning (*Enarxis*) of Sunday Matins (*Ὄρθρος*). The office opens with the Royal Beginning: the Trisagion Prayers, the Royal Troparia for the sovereign and the Church (*Σῶσον Κύριε*, *Ὁ ὑψωθεὶς ἐν τῷ Σταυρῷ*, *Προστασία φοβερά*), and the Litany of Fervent Supplication. After the sacerdotal doxology to the Holy Trinity, the Superior recites the Six Psalms (*Ἑξάψαλμος*, Psalms 3, 37, 62, 87, 102, 142 LXX). Violakis cites the ancient monastic typika (such as the Typikon of St. Sabas) demanding absolute stillness and reverence: *"all present listening in total silence and compunction, and let no one either by coughing or by any other disturbance disrupt the prayerful compunction, as the ancient Typika enjoin."* In patristic tradition, the Six Psalms symbolize the mystical quietude of the night and the Dread Judgment, during which the Priest stands uncovered before the Holy Doors reading the twelve Morning Prayers (*Ἑωθιναὶ Εὐχαί*). Following the Great Litany, the joyful enarxis of the day begins with **"God is the Lord, and hath appeared unto us"** (*Θεὸς Κύριος*, Psalm 117:27 LXX) chanted four times in the Tone of the day with its four appointed verses (Psalm 117:1, 10, 23 LXX). The Psalter stichologia follows, appointing the Kathismata of the Psalter and the Polyeleos (Psalms 134–135 LXX) for festal commemorations, or the 17th Kathisma (*Amomos*, Psalm 118 LXX) when no festal Polyeleos occurs.
[^128]: Distribution and Intercalation of Festal Sessional Hymns at Sunday Matins (Violakis, pp. 134–135, Note 2). In Note 2, Violakis clarifies the structural distribution of Sessional Hymns (*Καθίσματα*, Slavonic *Sedal'ny*) across the three Psalter readings (*Stichologiae*) of Sunday Matins: *"If the celebrated Saint is one of the more noted Saints of the Church, in each of the first two Stichologiae of the Kathismata we chant one Kathisma of the Saint, as noted in the particular services of these Saints"* (*Ἐὰν ὁ ἑορταζόμενος Ἅγιος ᾖ ἐκ τῶν ἐπισημοτέρων... καὶ ἐν ταῖς δυσὶ πρώταις στιχολογίαις τῶν Καθισμάτων ψάλλονται προσόμοια, ὡς ἐν ταῖς ἰδιαιτέραις αὐτῶν Ἀκολουθίαις σημειοῦται*). Under ordinary Sunday order, the first two Stichologiae (the Second and Third Kathismata of the Psalter) are followed exclusively by the Resurrection Sessional Hymns of the Octoechos with their proper Theotokia, while the hymnody of the Saint from the Menaion is relegated to the Third Stichologia (after the Polyeleos or Amomos).
